3.0°C
Implied Europe warming by 2100
4–5°C
Reference global trajectory: 2050
2.0°C
12
A recurring concern among attendees was a potential delay to the IPCC’s next assessment reports. The concern was twofold. First, the reports are needed to inform the second global

stocktake in 2028. Second, our discussions suggest there is concern that climate deterioration is happening faster than initially expected, thus delay risks decisions resting on out-of-
date science. Even if there is agreement to produce the reports in time, there is concern that it can’t be fully accurate, as the timeline may limit contributions from scientists in
developing countries, who are struggling to secure the funding and capacity needed to contribute within the shorter timeframe.

IPCC report timelines were a point of contention at COP31 discussions
The Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) is the UN body for assessing the scientific
evidence on climate change, and its assessment reports provide the scientific basis for climate
negotiations. The IPCC produces reports in 3 areas, namely 1) Physical Science, 2) Impacts,
Adaptation and Vulnerability, and 3) Mitigation, with the last such reports published prior to the

conclusion of the first global stocktake (which measures progress towards goals set out in the
Paris Agreement and informs the next round of national climate plans, known as NDCs) at COP28
in 2023. Coming into COP31, countries were aiming to agree on the timing for these reports
(having failed to reach agreement at previous IPCC meetings), such that they could be signed off at
the IPCC’s next meeting in December.
Europe pushed for faster IPCC reports, while other nations favoured delay

Typically, the IPCC reports have operated on a 5–7 year cycle, but the exact timings require
agreement among IPCC members. At COP31, European diplomats pushed for a trajectory that
would allow the next reports to inform the second global stocktake, which opened at COP31 and is
due to conclude at COP33 in 2028. Some nations favoured a longer timeline, with Kenya arguing
that scientists from developing countries needed more time to contribute, while India and Saudi

Arabia were also said to favour a delay. Some stakeholders at COP31 suggested the reports will
likely paint an alarming picture of the climate, and publishing them before the stocktake would
add pressure for stronger action.
Discussions will reconvene at December’s IPCC meetings
There is concern that agreement will not be reached in time. The longer the impasse continues,
the less time scientists have to prepare for publication. The IPCC Chair has previously been quoted

as stating that agreement must be reached by the end of 2026 if the reports are to be ready to
contribute to the second stocktake. Alongside (and somewhat linked to) disagreement over the
report timelines, nations will need to reach agreement on the IPCC’s budget for the remainder of
the current cycle.
IPCC reports and the global stocktake: key dates
Dec 2023 | COP28, Dubai
First global stocktake concludes; outcomes used to inform next round of
national climate plans

Nov 2026 | COP31, Antalya
Second global stocktake opens; countries again fail to agree a timeline for
the next IPCC reports
Dec 2026 | IPCC meeting
Discussions to reconvene; the IPCC Chair has said agreement is needed by
end-2026 for the reports to be ready in time to contribute to the 2nd
stocktake
Nov 2028 | COP33 (host unresolved)
Second global stocktake scheduled to conclude; outcome will inform the
next round of global climate plans.
Dec 2015 | COP21, Paris

Countries adopt the Paris Agreement. Article 14 establishes global
stocktakes every five years, unless parties agree otherwise.
Nov 2021 | COP26, Glasgow
First global stocktake begins, with information collection and preparation.
Apr 2022 | IPCC AR6 mitigation report released
IPCC publishes the 3rd AR6 working-group report, on mitigation of climate
change. This gave time for the findings of the 3 reports to contribute to the
first stocktake’s outcomes.

TAK EAW AY 6   |   TR AD E
Trade is still a point of contention

13
Cumulative value of EU imports from CBAM sectors by market (2015-24)
Source: BNEF, Deutsche Bank Research.
Note: Chart displays nominal value of cumulative imports to the EU by source markets.
Developing nations, including China, India and the African Group, pushed back against unilateral climate-related trade measures, with the EU’s Carbon Border Adjustment Mechanism
(CBAM) a particular target. We expect trade to remain a source of friction in climate diplomacy, while cleaner producers should gain a global trade advantage.

Several nations have sought to bring trade into climate discussions at COP
The African Group negotiating bloc submitted a request prior to the event to have an item on
climate-related trade measures formally added to the COP31 agenda. The attempt was not
successful. Meanwhile, both India and China came into COP31 promising to use the event to push
back against climate-related trade mechanisms, with the EU’s Carbon Border Adjustment
Mechanism (CBAM) a particular point of contention. Developed countries continue to defend such

measures, arguing that they are necessary to level the playing field for domestic firms in cases
where foreign competitors face lower environment-related compliance costs relative to domestic
industries.
At COP30, countries agreed to create a separate space away from COP to discuss climate-
related trade measures, yet this did not stop trade entering discussions
A senior member of China’s delegation described the new platform as “not enough”, calling for

stronger cooperation on measures including defining product carbon footprints and traceability of
agricultural goods to prevent deforestation. They also added that EU-China climate relations had
been damaged by trade tensions. Saudi Arabia and Russia also raised trade during COP31
discussions. A call in the first draft of the cover text “to phase out unilateral climate-related and
trade-restrictive measures” by the end of 2027 had no prospect of success, yet it reflects the desire

of developing countries to continue raising this topic at COP.
In some negotiating tracks, disputes over trade contributed to the collapse of discussions
In a sign of how prevalent trade was in Antalya, an EU official claimed that there was “no room” at
the talks where trade was not raised, describing this as “not helpful”. Indeed, trade was also cited
as the factor behind the collapse of COP31 negotiations on climate change and agriculture, after

some parties demanded that the talks discuss the EU’s CBAM due to claims over its impacts on
fertilisers.

### Page 14

Debbie Jones  |  COP31 Takeaways  |  November 2026
There was notable support for electrification goals
The COP Presidency positioned a new “35-by-35” electrification goal as a headline item
for COP31. The goal seeks to raise the share of electricity in global final energy use to
35% by 2035 (vs. c.23% currently) and was generally well received in Antalya, with more
than 80 countries reportedly endorsing it, including China and the EU.
No clean energy requirement drew scrutiny, but was a pragmatic step

As we highlighted in our COP31 preview, the lack of reference to clean energy drew
scrutiny, with concerns that the pledge would fail to deliver climate benefits if fossil fuels
were used to generate the additional electricity. Despite the EU’s eventual endorsement
of “35-by-35”, some member states were reportedly reluctant initially to sign up for this
reason. While we acknowledge these concerns, the pledge could also be viewed as a
well-crafted initiative that addresses energy security while building the infrastructure

needed for significant emissions reductions, even if not immediately. The absence of a
clean energy requirement (though it was implied) was also seen by some as a pragmatic
step that enabled wider participation, as reflected in the high number of nations
pledging support at COP31.
Despite the lack of a clean energy requirement, some countries still opposed the
inclusion of the “35-by-35” goal in the final cover decision
Some developing nations were against its inclusion on the grounds that they viewed it as

potentially restricting development choices. There was also opposition from some of
these countries against the imposition of top-down sectoral targets which do not make
allowances for varying national circumstances. Moreover, while the emphasis of the COP
Presidency on the initiative was likely a key factor behind why it received strong support,
some nations felt that this focus took away from other priorities at the event.

TAK EAW AY 7   |   EL ECTR I F I CATI ON
Electrification was the clearest common ground, with 80+ countries backing the “35-by-35” goal
14
Electrification was the clearest area of common ground in Antalya. The voluntary “35-by-35” pledge, which aims to raise electricity’s share of global final energy use to 35% by 2035, was
endorsed by more than 80 countries, including China and the EU, and drew strong business support. The pledge does not formally require clean energy, which drew criticism.

However, we viewed the ambiguity as a way to facilitate broader agreement and grow the infrastructure needed for a more meaningful shift away from fossil fuels. The effort also
aligned with broader energy security goals. We expect continued focus on this topic at future COP events.
Business engagement on electrification at COP31 was high
This was evident in the COP31 Electrification Delivery Package, which sought to highlight
billions in investment to support areas including grids and the electrification of key

sectors such as transport and industry. The UN highlighted that more than 260
businesses with more than $4tn in annual revenue “announced or profiled” actions in
support of these objectives at COP31. Meanwhile, the Utilities for Net Zero Alliance
(UNEZA) announced combined 2026 annual investment plans across grids, renewables
and storage of at least $156bn, a 5% increase on 2025 and a 35% rise vs. 2024.

(2025 vs. 2035 IEA scenarios and the COP31 35-by-35 goal)

### Page 15

Debbie Jones  |  COP31 Takeaways  |  November 2026
AI was a missed opportunity, with just 2% of Blue Zone events focused on it
15
AI was a missed opportunity. While the power demands of data centres were acknowledged as a driver of increased energy demand, discussion of the role AI could play in advancing
climate goals was surprisingly limited. Beyond the formal negotiations, COP is also a major educational and climate technology forum, and we had expected the potential for AI to help

accelerate progress towards net zero to feature far more prominently. The Presidency’s Antalya AI Pledge was a welcome step, but drew support from only seven governments, while
support from major AI companies was also limited.
AI-related discussions weren’t as prevalent as we expected
It is unclear whether this reflects fundamental uncertainty among companies about how to scale
AI sustainably or a lack of understanding of how powerful AI could be in solving climate challenges.

In our view, it is likely a combination of both, making this a missed opportunity for COP to educate
companies and governments. That is not to say there weren’t some great AI panels. At one AI
innovation panel we attended, McKinsey noted that AI sales forecasting can help retailers match
ordering to demand and cut avoidable food waste, reducing emissions (roughly a third of food is
never eaten) and delivering monetary savings.
Only 2% of COP31 Blue Zone events featured a notable focus on AI

As a check against our COP experience, we analysed the focus of more than 4,000 panels and
events listed as being held in the Blue Zone at COP31 (those we could gather from the COP31
programme available on the official website). Our AI driven analysis assessed the focus of each
session and determined a maximum of 3 focus topics. Our analysis showed that AI had the lowest
focus of any of the listed topics, with just 2% of COP31 Blue Zone events having a notable focus on

AI. As expected, climate finance and investment was the most frequently addressed topic.
The COP31 Presidency did put AI on the Action Agenda
Türkiye launched the Antalya AI Pledge on 16 November, recognising AI both as a tool for climate
action and a growing source of environmental pressure. Developed with the ITU, this voluntary
framework asks signatories to select measurable commitments, such as powering AI infrastructure
with low- and zero-emission energy and reporting AI’s environmental footprint. By 19 November,

only seven governments had endorsed it, with little support from major AI players, Alibaba being
the notable exception. This could reflect concern that AI will challenge firms’ near-term emission
commitments, especially for tech companies. Here we would highlight a conversation we had with
a leading Chinese solar panel company. When asked how China would handle sustainability issues
from the expected rapid build-out of data centres, an attendee we spoke to said matter of factly:

“China will make data centres sustainable”.

### Page 16

Debbie Jones  |  COP31 Takeaways  |  November 2026
TAK EAW AY 9   |   U NI TED  S TATES
The US federal absence weighs on COP, but the midterms boost state-level momentum
16
The absence of the US federal government under the second Trump administration has had a notable negative impact on negotiations and signaling around financing. The Democrats
continue to show up at COP, this year with a message of optimism following the midterm election results. The America Is All In coalition held several panels at COP highlighting climate

commitments and objectives. Democratic governors showed up to remind COP attendees that roughly three-quarters of the US population now have Democratic governors,
strengthening the case that state-level policy will be a key driver of US clean energy and industrial investment. While we tend to agree, the magnitude of the benefit isn’t entirely clear.
The US once again had no federal presence at COP
Explaining the decision to stay away for a second straight COP, a spokesperson for the federal

administration said it was focused on expanding “affordable, reliable, secure energy sources”. This
also meant the US had no formal negotiating presence, though the climate pivot under the second
Trump administration potentially emboldened nations opposed to faster transition timelines.
US State representation and companies did come to Antalya
At the America Is All In pavilion we attended the US Climate Alliance panel “Undaunted”, featuring
Governors Michelle Lujan Grisham (New Mexico) and Tim Walz (Minnesota). Key messages:

– States retain sufficient regulatory authority, economic influence and private sector partnerships
to keep advancing clean energy, industrial decarbonisation and climate resilience, with member
states of the alliance having reduced net greenhouse gas emissions by nearly a quarter
between 2005 and 2024 while also growing their economies by 37%.
– Clean electricity was presented as an industrial competitiveness issue, with manufacturers
demanding reliable access to clean power when deciding where to locate production.

– Affordability and employment were recurring themes, with both governors stressing that
climate policy must deliver visible economic benefits.
– Methane was a focus. New Mexico argued it has combined rising oil and gas output with
stringent methane standards and super-emitter monitoring, resulting in lower emissions.
Governor Grisham signalled hopes of encouraging Texas, which has just elected Democrat Gina
Hinojosa as governor, to adopt similar rules.

– Governor Walz of Minnesota criticised companies that publicly support climate action but locate
manufacturing in states with weaker labour and environmental policies.
– Consistent with our wider observations (see takeaway 8), AI was notably absent from the
discussion, with data centres mentioned only in passing.

In the US (and abroad), the level of alarm over global warming is inching higher
As part of our monthly Climate & Sustainability Survey, we ask consumers in the UK, US, Germany, France, Spain and Italy for their views on global warming. In Q3 2026, we recorded a notable
rebound in alarm regarding the pace of global warming, particularly in the UK and Europe following a summer of climate extremes. The level of alarm had begun to dip globally post the 2024 US

presidential election in November.

### Page 18

Debbie Jones  |  COP31 Takeaways  |  November 2026
TAK EAW AY 1 0   |   NATU R E, CAR BON MAR K ETS  & OCEANS
Continued scientist alarm over nature and ocean health raises the importance of scaling financing mechanisms
18
Nature and the oceans returned to the agenda at COP31, a month after the UN’s biodiversity summit (CBD COP17) found most 2030 targets off track. Scientists warned of record
ocean heat, and with an overshoot of 1.5°C looking ever more likely, the case for scaling up finance is growing stronger. Canada’s pledge of up to $392.5mn to the Tropical Forest

Forever Facility (TFFF), launched at COP30, gave forest finance a lift, while Brazil presented a deforestation roadmap outside the formal talks. In carbon markets, the focus has moved
from writing the Article 6 rules to making them work. Buyers want high-integrity credits, governments want faster payments. In our view, buyer confidence must come first.
Scientists again warned of concerns over the health of oceans and nature
The UN held a dedicated biodiversity summit (CBD COP17) in Armenia last month. It

found that progress towards most of the 2030 targets under the Kunming-Montreal
Biodiversity Framework has been insufficient. Scientists made similar points at COP31.
The WMO’s 2026 “State of the Climate” report, published during the summit, said ocean
heat content in the upper 2,000 metres reached a record in 2025, with preliminary data
pointing to further increases in 2026.
The value of these systems is well understood, but financing to support their

protection remains a challenge
On a global level, the economic case for protecting these assets is clear, with the World
Bank estimating that more than half of global GDP depends on nature and its services,
while studies put the value of the ocean economy at between $3-6tn. Developing
countries state that while they want to protect oceans and nature, this can often be at
odds with national economic development goals. Finance flows for conservation and

protection initiatives remain relatively small compared with projects in areas such as
clean energy, while developing countries cite misalignment between their needs and
financing conditions, with accessibility a further challenge.
Scaling the financial sector’s involvement will be key
Our analysis shows 2026 is already a record year for blue bond issuance, though it
remains a fraction of green bond issuance. The Ocean Risk and Resilience Action Alliance

(ORRAA), which funds coastal resilience, secured new commitments at COP31, with
Canada pledging CAD$14mn over four years and Australia AUD$1mn over two.
Mangrove Breakthrough, which backs mangrove protection (coastal forests that shield
shorelines, store carbon and support fisheries), said its tracked large-scale projects
reached almost US$1bn in cumulative value since 2020, up 32% year on year.
On carbon markets, implementation is now the main focus for Article 6

Carbon markets let countries and companies offset emissions by paying for projects that
cut or remove them elsewhere. Article 6 of the Paris Agreement sets UN-backed rules for
such trading, and most technical details were settled at earlier COPs. COP31 left the
rulebook intact, with a review due in 2028, so attention turned to implementation. On 16
November the UN climate secretariat soft-launched the first version of an international
registry for Article 6.2, the country-to-country track (Article 6.4 is the centralised UN

mechanism). This international version lets countries without their own registry record
and track credit transfers.
Several Article 6 deals were announced
Norway agreed to buy up to 12 million tonnes of emissions reductions from solar
projects run by Indonesia’s state utility PLN through 2038, at a reported US$20–40 a
tonne. It pays only once reductions are verified and delivered, funding projects that
would otherwise not be viable. Separately, Singapore approved its first Article 6.2 project,

in Rwanda, which will distribute 300,000 ceramic water filters so households can purify
water without boiling it over firewood or charcoal. It is expected to avoid about 780,000
tonnes of CO₂e in 2026–30.
For carbon markets, buyer confidence should remain the priority
Fourteen governments signed the Antalya Statement on Mobilising Private Finance
for Climate Action on 12 November, with a playbook of eight policy levers to grow
corporate demand for high-integrity credits. Corporates and investors still stress the

need for robust credits that deliver the promised emissions reductions and limit
greenwashing risk, while some governments want faster payments and less bureaucracy.
Campaigners warn that lobbying to loosen the rules will intensify before the 2028 review.
In our view, buyer confidence in credit trading processes is paramount and should
remain the priority.
Note: Deutsche Bank is a member of the Ocean Risk and Resilience Action Alliance (ORRAA).

### Page 19

Debbie Jones  |  COP31 Takeaways  |  November 2026
F U TU R E O F  C O P
Ethiopia will host COP32 in 2027, though questions over how COP will evolve are in focus
COP has shifted from setting targets to implementation. Ethiopia will host COP32 in 2027, where we expect a focus on delivering commitments and climate finance access, reflecting African
priorities. COP33 falls to Asia-Pacific in 2028, though the host remains unconfirmed at present. COP32 and COP33 will test how well multilateralism can respond to the setback at COP31. With a

second Global Stocktake concluding in 2028, its outcome should inform countries’ next national climate plans (NDCs, their emissions-reduction pledges), due in 2030.

AP P END I X  |   COP  TER MS
Appendix A: Key COP terms used in this report
This appendix explains the main COP process terms and negotiating concepts used throughout the report. Definitions are summarised for non-specialist readers and reflect how each term is
used in this deck.
20
TERM DEFINITION
35-by-35 pledge Türkiye’s voluntary goal to lift electricity’s share of global final energy use to 35% by 2035; endorsed by 80+ countries.

Action Agenda Voluntary, non-binding pledges and initiatives launched alongside the formal talks. It is separate from negotiated decisions.
Annex II / Non-Annex I UNFCCC country classification. Annex II covers developed economies; Non-Annex I covers most other countries, including China and India.
Article 6 Paris Agreement framework including rules on international carbon markets. Article 6.2 covers bilateral trading of credits between countries.

Article 9
Paris Agreement finance provisions. Article 9.1 covers developed
countries’ obligation to provide finance. COP30 set a two-year work
programme for 2026-27.
Baku to Belém Roadmap
to $1.3T
Roadmap to scale climate finance to $1.3tn a year by 2035 from all
sources, inclusive of the $300bn NCQG.
Blue Zone UN-controlled area of the COP venue for formal negotiations, pavilions and side events.
Bonn sessions Mid-year technical talks of the UNFCCC Subsidiary Bodies in Bonn, Germany, which prepare the ground for COP discussions.

Brazil fossil fuel roadmap COP30 Presidency’s voluntary global roadmap for transitioning away from fossil fuels, pursued outside the formal UN negotiations.
CBAM Carbon Border Adjustment Mechanism: EU levy on imports of carbon- intensive goods such as steel, cement, fertiliser and aluminium.
Coalitions of the willing Like-minded countries acting outside the consensus process, such as the 50+ nation Santa Marta process on fossil fuel roadmaps.
Consensus How COP decisions are taken: adopted when no party formally objects, so a single party can theoretically block or dilute an outcome.

COP / COPs Conference of the Parties: the UNFCCC’s annual decision-making summit, where nearly 200 parties negotiate climate action.
COP Presidency The host country’s team that chairs the conference. For COP31, Türkiye’s Murat Kurum was COP President and led the Action Agenda.
Final COP text / cover text Headline decisions adopted by consensus at the close of the COP, setting its formal outcome. Also called the cover decision.
TERM DEFINITION
Formal agenda Negotiated items on the official COP agenda, decided by consensus. Separate from the voluntary Action Agenda.

Global Goal on
Adaptation (GGA)
Paris Agreement goal to strengthen resilience. COP30 adopted 59
indicators; the process to refine these moved forward at COP31.
Global South Informal term for developing and emerging economies, mainly in Africa, Asia and Latin America.
Global Stocktake (GST) Five-yearly review of progress under the Paris Agreement. The second runs from COP31 to COP33 (2028).
IPCC Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change: the UN body that assesses climate science. Its reports feed the Global Stocktake.

Just transition
mechanism
Belém Action Mechanism agreed at COP30, focused on fairness in the
shift to a low-carbon economy.
Loss and damage fund Fund set up at COP27 and operationalised in 2023 to support vulnerable developing countries.
NCQG New Collective Quantified Goal (COP29): at least $300bn a year for developing countries by 2035, within a $1.3tn ambition.
NDC Nationally determined contribution: each country’s national climate plan, with emissions targets and actions, updated every five years.

Negotiating groups Blocs coordinating national positions: G77 & China, EU, African and Arab Groups, LDCs (least developed) and AOSIS (small island states).
Paris Agreement 2015 treaty to hold warming well below 2°C and pursue 1.5°C, with national climate plans (NDCs) as its core mechanism.
President of Negotiations COP31 role held by Australia’s Chris Bowen, who drafted final texts and steered government-to-government talks.
Temperature overshoot Global warming exceeding the 1.5°C Paris limit, temporarily or permanently, which science now suggests is likely.

UAE Consensus COP28 (Dubai, 2023) agreement to “transition away from fossil fuels”, the first such call in a COP text.
UNFCCC UN Framework Convention on Climate Change: the 1992 treaty behind the climate process. Its parties meet annually at the COP.

## Document 328: European Economics Analyst_ UK Outlook 2027_ Cyclical Headwinds, Strengthening Fundamentals

Source PDF: all report pdf/European Economics Analyst_ UK Outlook 2027_ Cyclical Headwinds, Strengthening Fundamentals.pdf
Report source: Goldman Sachs Publishing
Date: 2026-11-15
Pages: 14
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

The UK economy proved surprisingly resilient in 2026. Growth accelerated n
despite rising energy prices and tighter financial conditions, and the labour 
market showed signs of stabilisation. The new government navigated a difficult 
fiscal backdrop to deliver a low-key budget that maintained the consolidation 
path. 
The coming quarters are likely to be more challenging. First, we expect growth to n
slow as the impact of the energy shock on consumer spending increases over the 
winter. Second, inflation will rise further as electricity and gas bills increase and 
the indirect effects of the energy shock build. We expect this to push the BoE to 
hike a second time, probably in February. Third, the government faces a difficult 
Spending Review; existing spending plans look too tight, but the Chancellor has 
limited fiscal headroom. 
Nonetheless, we see compelling reasons to be constructive on the economic n
outlook for 2027. First, some of the recent growth improvement looks structural, 
and the productivity trend is strengthening. We therefore expect activity to 
reaccelerate later in the year as some of the cyclical headwinds fade, and our 
Q4/Q4 growth forecast is above consensus at 1.4%. Second, we do not expect 
strong second-round effects from the energy shock into domestic inflation, so 
the BoE is likely to hike significantly less than priced. Third, we expect further 
fiscal consolidation, and a more favourable OBR forecast could ease the 
trade-offs facing the Chancellor. 
We also see scope for sentiment around the UK’s longer-term economic n
prospects to improve. The UK is well-placed to benefit from AI adoption given its 
strengths in professional services, and surveys indicate that companies expect a 
material boost to productivity growth in the coming years. The PM has also 
recently raised the possibility of closer EU ties, which could bring notable 
economic gains over time.
James Moberly

### Page 2

UK Outlook 2027: Cyclical Headwinds, Strengthening Fundamentals

The UK economy proved surprisingly resilient in 2026. Despite the energy shock keeping 
inflation elevated and causing the BoE to pivot from cutting to hiking, growth 
accelerated this year. The labour market showed signs of improvement, with 
unemployment falling and employment growth indicators ticking up. The new 
government navigated a challenging fiscal backdrop to deliver a low-key Budget that 
maintained the consolidation path.

Cyclical Headwinds 
The coming quarters are likely to prove more challenging in several respects. First, we 
expect growth to slow in the near term and run slightly below trend in Q4 and the first 
half of 2027. The impact of the energy shock on household finances is likely to build over 
the winter, with weak real disposable income growth resulting in a slowdown in 
consumer spending.

Exhibit 1: Growth Proved Surprisingly Resilient in 2026

Source: Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research, Haver Analytics, Bank of England
15 November 2026   2
Goldman Sachs European Economics Analyst

### Page 3

Tighter financial conditions should also weigh on growth as mortgages are refixed at 
higher rates. Fiscal policy will likely turn more contractionary compared with this year 
given that current plans imply a larger consolidation in the primary balance in 2027.

While the labour market data improved over the last few months, we expect slower 
near-term growth to result in a modest further weakening. That is consistent with 
continued softness in most indicators of employment intentions. The pace of loosening 
should be gradual as the peak impact of last year’s labour cost increases on employment 
growth has probably passed. But we see the unemployment rate rising back to 5% by the 
middle of next year.

Exhibit 2: Consumer Spending Growth Likely to Slow

Source: Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research, Haver Analytics

Source: Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research, Haver Analytics, Bloomberg, OBR

### Page 4

Second, inflation is likely to increase further in the coming months. Consumer energy 
prices will rise notably in January on the back of higher electricity and gas bills. We also 
expect food and core goods inflation to accelerate as the indirect effects of the energy 
shock become more apparent. We also see renewed upward pressure in some regulated 
services prices that are indexed to headline inflation, and higher fuel costs will likely raise 
transport services prices. Overall, we expect inflation to peak at 4.3% in February and 
average 4.1% in 2027H1.

The peak in headline inflation will likely exceed the threshold at which the BoE has 
typically become more concerned about second-round effects. Taken together with 
hawkish recent commentary, that points to another 25bp BoE hike to 4.25%. This will 
probably come in February, although an earlier December move is also possible.

Exhibit 4: Renewed Labour Market Softness

Source: Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research, Haver Analytics, Bank of England

Exhibit 5: Rising Inflationary Pressures Point to a Second BoE Hike

The forecasts shown on the left are conditional on GS forecasts for energy prices.

Source: Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research, Haver Analytics, Bank of England
15 November 2026   4
Goldman Sachs European Economics Analyst

### Page 5

Third, the government faces a difficult Spending Review. Existing plans imply a notable 
slowdown in departmental spending growth from next year onwards, which looks 
challenging to deliver given pressures on the health and defence budgets. History 
suggests that these plans will probably be notably upgraded. New policy priorities could 
require further outlays, with PM Burnham’s agenda likely to become clearer after the 
publication of his 10-Year Plan. But financing higher spending may be challenging given 
limited fiscal headroom, with revenues constrained by Labour’s manifesto not to raise 
taxes on working people. Welfare reforms could bring some savings over time but look 
likely to be accompanied by upfront investment in employment support.

Structural Improvement 
Despite these cyclical challenges, we see several reasons to be more constructive on the 
UK’s economic prospects for 2027. First, some of the tailwinds that supported growth 
this year look structural. Strength in business investment has partly reflected rising 
information and communications capex, which looks likely to be sustained by ongoing 
data centre investment. This investment should support continued growth in technology 
services output, where demand has increased on the back of AI adoption. Further 
growth in utilities capital spending linked to renewables should also continue to support 
demand.

Exhibit 6: A Challenging Spending Review

Source: OBR
15 November 2026   5
Goldman Sachs European Economics Analyst

### Page 6

The productivity trend also appears to be improving, as growth in output per worker has 
increased significantly recently. Some of this may reflect the impact of last year’s 
employer National Insurance hike on hiring, but more recent data also show gains in 
sectors such as professional services that were less affected by labour cost increases. We 
also see more fundamental reasons to expect a pickup. While Brexit has probably 
weighed significantly on growth over the past decade, the worst of the headwind has 
likely passed. Survey data from the Decision Maker Panel indicate that early gains from 
AI adoption could also have modestly boosted the figures.

These factors, together with declines in energy prices, should mean that growth 
reaccelerates through the second half of next year. Overall, we expect a 1.4% Q4/Q4 
increase in GDP, above consensus expectations as well as the BoE and OBR forecasts.

Exhibit 7: Some Growth Drivers Look More Structural

Source: Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research, Haver Analytics, Yotzov et al. (2026)

Source: Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research, Haver Analytics, Various Academic Studies

### Page 7

Second, we continue to expect limited second-round effects into domestic price 
pressures. Even after the recent decline in the unemployment rate, broader measures of 
utilisation (such as the vacancy/unemployment ratio) suggest that the labour market 
remains far looser than in 2022/23. That will likely reduce the extent to which higher 
headline inflation passes through into wage pressures. Indeed, early data indicate that 
pay settlements are likely to remain stable next year, and the BoE’s Decision Maker Panel 
survey shows little change in wage expectations since the start of the Middle East 
conflict. Private sector regular pay growth will probably accelerate from low levels but is 
likely to remain well below the levels seen after the previous energy shock.

With only a modest increase in pay growth, we expect overall labour cost inflation to be 
well below the levels seen in recent years. This is especially true in consumer-facing 
sectors, which saw sharper increases in wages over recent years and the largest impact 
from the employer National Insurance increases. More moderate labour cost inflation

higher energy and food prices.

Given moderate second-round effects, we think that the BoE will hike less than priced. 
After tightening policy in February, we expect the MPC to keep Bank Rate at 4.25% for 
the rest of the year, whereas the market implies two further rate increases. The risks 
around our baseline are skewed towards further tightening, but even on a 
probability-weighted basis our forecast is notably below current market pricing.

Third, 2027 is likely to see renewed progress on deficit reduction. The current fiscal 
plans imply a steep reduction in the primary deficit next year. While we think spending 
could overshoot the official projections, tax receipts could also come in somewhat 
higher. Further consolidation should mean that the UK continues to compare favourably 
to other advanced economies in terms of reducing borrowing. While the Spending

A Positive Long-Run Outlook 
We also see scope for sentiment around the UK’s longer-term economic prospects to 
improve. The UK is well-placed to benefit from AI adoption given its specialisation in 
high-value added services. IMF estimates indicate that a relatively high share of the 
workforce is exposed to AI, with a particularly large number of workers in roles that may 
see productivity gains but are less likely to be displaced. Survey data suggest that firms 
expect adoption to result in sizeable productivity increases in the coming years. Even 
though these gains are unlikely to be fully additive to the current productivity trend, the 
numbers still indicate upside risks to our estimates for supply growth.

Exhibit 13: Continued Fiscal Consolidation

On the left, the UK data are shown on a public sector basis.

Source: Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research, OBR, Bank of England

Goldman Sachs European Economics Analyst

### Page 10

The UK is also well-positioned in several high growth innovative industries. This includes 
a world-leading position in life sciences, such as pharmaceuticals, biotechnology, 
medical technology, and genomics. The UK has a world-class research base, with the 
London/Oxford/Cambridge triangle a leading biotech cluster. 
Another possible source of long-run growth upside would be closer ties with the EU. The 
upcoming (November 20) summit could see some progress, with the two sides expected 
to finalise agrifood and emissions trading deals. But the Prime Minister has also 
indicated that he will use the summit to outline options for how the relationship might 
develop after the next election, potentially including a customs union, rejoining the 
single market, or even a second referendum on membership. Any reset would require 
negotiation, political trade-offs, and time before benefits materialise. But it could bring 
significant economic upside, so signals towards improved trading links could start to lift 
optimism on the UK’s growth prospects.

## Document 329: European resilience, American vigour (2026-10-30)

Source PDF: all report pdf/European resilience, American vigour (2026-10-30).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-10-30
Pages: 2
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Paris, 30 October 2026

EcoFlash

European resilience, American vigour

Eurozone growth continues to prove resilient, notwithstanding the volatility of the Irish figures. The Q3 data reinforce 
our forecast of 1.1% growth this year, which could even be slightly exceeded, as German and Spanish momentum shows 
no sign of fading. In France, the negative surprise (-0.1% q/q against a consensus of +0.1% q/q) stems from a new one-
off factor (the sharp fall in aerospace output caused by delays in input deliveries) and masks an encouraging rebound 
in household consumption. In the United States, growth reached its highest level since Q2 2025 (0.8% q/q), driven by 
buoyant private domestic demand.

Eurozone: The resilience of growth is confirmed 
Eurozone GDP grew by 0.4% q/q in Q3 according to Eurostat's preliminary flash estimate, above both the consensus and our 
Nowcast (0.3%). The ever-volatile Irish figures contributed 0.2 pp to this outcome. Excluding Ireland, growth (0.2% q/q) 
nonetheless remains in line with the pace of recent quarters (0.2-0.3% q/q on average). This illustrates the Eurozone's capacity 
to withstand the current shocks, thanks in particular to the strength of Spain (+0.07 pp contribution to the q/q change in Eurozone’s 
GDP) and the Netherlands (+0.04 pp), and to Germany's gradual recovery (+0.06 pp). France (-0.03 pp) and Italy (zero growth), 
by contrast, posted disappointing results, although in France's case these partly reflect temporary factors (aerospace output). The 
growth carry-over for 2026 now stands at 1.0%, which supports our forecast of 1.1% in annual average terms and even points to 
upside risk. The European Commission's October surveys remain upbeat, with a further improvement in industrial activity 
expectations. 
The German recovery continues. As anticipated by our Nowcast, real GDP grew by 0.2% q/q in Q3 (after 0.3% in Q2). 
According to Destatis, consumption (private and public) increased slightly, while investment edged down. Export momentum 
slowed markedly compared with Q2. This is Germany's fourth consecutive quarter of growth. 
Italian GDP surprised on the downside, stagnating in Q3 (against a consensus of 0.1%). According to Istat, this reflects a 
decline in value added in industry, offset by increases in services and agriculture. The positive contribution from domestic demand 
was reportedly cancelled out by a negative contribution from net trade. 
Spanish growth remained robust in Q3, in line with expectations (0.6% q/q, after 0.7% in Q2), driven mainly by domestic 
demand (0.5 pp contribution) and, to a lesser extent, by net exports (0.1 pp). Consumption remains the main engine of growth 
(+0.6% q/q for private consumption and +0.9% for public consumption). Investment rose by 0.7%. Export growth (1.3%) once 
again outpaced import growth (1.1%) this quarter. All sectors except construction recorded positive changes in value added, with 
industry and services both up 0.7%. 
France: A sharp drop in aerospace output prevents a return to growth in Q3 
GDP contracted by 0.1% q/q in Q3 (after stagnating in Q2), weighed down by a sharp fall in aerospace output (-4.5% q/q 
in the production of other transport equipment, which includes aerospace). This steep decline dragged down goods production 
(down 1.2% q/q) and explains the sharp contraction in exports (-1.7% q/q) and business investment (-0.4% q/q). Net trade thus 
subtracted 0.6 pp from GDP (imports were flat over the same period). Conversely, household consumption surprised on the 
upside (+0.4% q/q), boosted by purchases of electric vehicles (registrations up 112% y/y in Q3). Public consumption 
(+0.3% q/q) also supported growth, as did activity in market services (+0.3% q/q). Despite the sharp drop in aerospace 
output in Q3, Airbus has confirmed its delivery targets for the year (with engine supply problems expected to be resolved), pointing 
to a strong rebound in growth in Q4 (0.3% q/q according to our forecasts). Overall, growth is expected to reach 0.3% in 2026, 
before picking up to 0.8% in 2027. 
United States: Growth at a one-year high  
GDP growth picked up in Q3 to 3.4% at a seasonally adjusted annualised rate (AR; i.e. 0.8% q/q), from 2.2% in Q2 
(0.6% q/q). This is the fastest pace since Q3 2025, confirming that activity is almost immune to the energy shock. Private domestic 
demand remains particularly strong. Private non-residential investment accelerated to 12.7% AR, a performance unmatched since 
the post-Covid reopening. AI-related components are driving this figure (18.5% AR), but other types of investment are also rising 
for a second consecutive quarter (3.8% AR) after contracting uninterruptedly throughout 2025. Household consumption, steady 
at 2.5% AR in real terms, is growing faster than it did before inflation picked up again, but at the cost of a significant drop in the 
saving rate (3.8% at the end of the quarter, down from 4.4% at the end of the previous quarter). The United States nonetheless 
continues to "import its growth": net trade is weighing on GDP owing to a surge in imports (19.7% AR), driven by AI-related 
components – IT, semiconductors and telecommunications (103.8% AR).

Goldman Sachs Global Markets Outlook 2027

### Page 3

Markets Outlook 2027: Growing Pains

1. A maturing market cycle 
Stable growth, declining inflation make for a friendly macro backdrop. n
But risks are to the downside, and an ageing market cycle is facing more headwinds. n
Higher rates, wider spreads, and a peaking AI impulse mean the broad asset picture n
is more two-sided ... 
... and argues for more diversified allocations, including bonds and hedges. n
Our headline macro forecasts for 2027 are quite friendly: stable growth, declining 
inflation and peaking policy rates. But as the economic cycle extends, the market cycle is 
increasingly showing its age. For 2026, our view was that even though asset market 
valuations were running ahead of the macro, a positive cyclical backdrop including 
strong investment growth should dominate. While the Iran war has upended many 
forecasts, even without the energy shock the market is running into more conventional 
late-cycle headwinds. Real rates are higher, credit spreads are wider and volatility is 
picking up, especially under the surface. On top of this, the AI impulse, which has been a 
key support to markets through the shocks this year, looks poised to peak in the coming 
year. 
That is likely to prove a bigger headwind to the profit and asset market picture than 
growth, given its larger role in supporting equity markets. Markets have already moved 
some way towards reflecting that the extraordinary surge in profitability in the AI 
infrastructure complex is unlikely to be extrapolated. So a flattening in the gains there 
may be offset by gains further down the waterfall among AI users or other cyclical parts 
of the market, especially if energy prices and rates fall more. But that is a tricky 
balancing act, and further boosts to equity prices are likely to come alongside a higher 
base level of volatility. There are also risks that AI-related drawdowns tighten financial 
conditions more meaningfully, or that energy and policy relief do not arrive on time. 
That leaves the broader risk-asset picture looking more two-sided than it has for several 
years, arguing for more diversified and better-hedged portfolios. 
12 November 2026   3
Goldman Sachs Global Markets Outlook 2027

### Page 4

2. The AI investment impulse peaks 
The AI investment impulse peak is a bigger test for markets than the economy. n
That impulse has been a larger contributor to growth in market value and earnings n
than in GDP. 
Markets have already de-rated AI infrastructure beneficiaries. n
Valuation excesses are less visible, so the focus is on sustainability of record profits. n
Coming into 2026, we expected continued acceleration in AI investment to dominate 
valuation concerns in equities, but likely amid higher volatility and wider credit spreads.  
Although we expect solid AI investment growth to continue, our forecasts imply that the 
investment growth impulse—additional AI investment as a share of GDP—will peak in 
early 2027. From a macro perspective, this should be a manageable event. But it may be 
a bigger test for markets for three reasons. First, the AI impulse has been a much larger 
contributor to growth in earnings and market value than to growth in GDP. Second, 
AI-related equities have taken credit for significant macro productivity gains, especially 
companies benefiting directly from the infrastructure boom, that have made them more 
vulnerable to any signs that we are on a less optimistic path. Third, the point at which 
growth rates peak (a “second derivative” turning point) often matters, even if underlying 
growth rates (the “first derivative”) remain healthy. Moving through that peak allows 
markets to place limits on the upside of an uncertain distribution and, as growth 
decelerates, the period of positive surprises to expectations often ends too, as we may 
be starting to see for the AI capex cycle. 
Markets have already been reflecting these concerns. Since June, we have seen a 
significant de-rating of companies levered to the AI infrastructure theme, which are 
most sensitive to shifts in the investment outlook and had been the biggest market 
beneficiaries in the first half of 2026. Moreover, robust profits, and a rising share of

income going to listed companies, have helped keep both the imbalances and the deep 
valuation excesses that we saw in the late 1990s at bay. The key question in this cycle is 
whether the market is correctly assessing how much of the recent acceleration in profits, 
which has taken S&P 500 earnings to a record high relative to GDP, will prove persistent, 
especially as the investment cycle itself fades. Without the capital inflows from the EM 
crises in 1997 and 1998—or the rate cuts that goods price deflation allowed during that 
tech cycle—increased financing needs have also been putting clearer upward pressure 
on the cost of capital.

3. Passing—or dropping—the baton 
Strong levels of AI investment growth and profitability should limit the pressure. n
And the baton may be passed from AI infrastructure to other market areas. n
But we see risks of a larger aggregate shock if the market looks forward, or other n
areas cannot take up the slack. 
Tighter financial conditions or credit conditions could create a feedback loop. n
Two things give us comfort in terms of how bumpy this transition through the peak of 
the investment cycle will prove to be. The first is that although the investment impulse 
from AI looks poised to peak, growth in both AI investment and earnings should remain 
firmly positive in 2027, especially earlier in the year. We expect S&P 500 earnings growth 
to decelerate from 36% in 2026 to a still-robust 11% in 2027. This may ultimately help to 
limit the de-rating of even the most directly affected areas and should reduce the 
impact on broader growth-sensitive assets, though it should also imply less relief in core 
rates pricing. The second is that the baton may be passed, at least to a degree, from the 
direct beneficiaries of the AI investment boom to other areas of the market. This 
includes other areas of the AI complex, such as the hyperscalers, if monetization paths 
become more visible; companies that may begin to show benefits from AI productivity 
enhancements; or non-AI companies that see some relief as the crowding out of capital

available to them eases. 
The risk is that what could be more of a rotation—and a flattening in the upward trend in 
asset markets—becomes a bigger aggregate shock. This could happen because the 
market begins to look further forward to the peak in the investment share itself (in 2028 
on our forecasts); because the passing of the baton to other sectors does not take place 
as quickly or as smoothly as needed; or because feedback loops from financial and 
credit conditions accelerate more quickly than in our baseline. We think the risk to 
markets is higher than the risk to the economic picture on each front, because the boom 
itself has had its largest impact there. For that reason, the most likely channel through 
which AI worries could lead to broader economic downside is through financial 
conditions themselves.

4. Energy—balancing risk and risk premium 
An escalation-driven oil spike remains one of the largest near-term risks to markets. n
Our baseline is for a gradual decline in energy prices, but the hedge value of energy n
is still high. 
Downside tail to energy prices with clearer/faster resolution. n
Risk premium has fluctuated, so hedge value also varies over time. n
A potential oil price spike driven by another escalation in the war with Iran remains one 
of the biggest near-term risks to the markets and growth picture. Particularly after the 
fall in oil prices so far in November, pricing is vulnerable to any broadening in the conflict 
and new supply disruptions. We would expect that to hurt equities, support the Dollar 
against European currencies and put upward pressure on rates, all else equal. Europe, 
too, faces a precarious winter, given low storage levels in natural gas which leave the 
region vulnerable to risks from both supply and weather. If those risks are avoided, our 
baseline is for a gradual decline in energy prices over the coming year even without full 
resolution of the conflict. Despite the ongoing war with Iran, oil market supply has 
largely been restored (even if refining capacity is limited), but risks of escalation and 
further disruptions have generally led the market to demand a fluctuating but 
meaningful risk premium above where we think the fundamental supply-demand picture 
would leave them.

### Page 7

Our baseline is a gradual recovery in supply and reduction in energy prices, leaving 
energy and core commodity returns more muted. But the risk of more serious 
disruptions is real, and energy prices could move significantly higher in those scenarios 
at a time when equities and, potentially, bonds could both be under pressure. The 
hedging value of the asset class remains high for that reason. The wrinkle is that the risk 
premium in oil has varied substantially and conditions the value of the hedge. When the 
risk premium falls, as it has done more recently, that hedging value is likely to be higher. 
When that risk premium is very high, as it was at the end of October, oil markets are 
more vulnerable to downside tails if news flow is better than expected, and outright 
longs (rather than options) have generally been more risky. The downside tail from faster 
progress towards resolution than we expect (not just in Iran, but in Ukraine too) is one of 
the clearest sources of upside risks for broader markets. In that scenario, energy prices 
could come down more quickly, supporting both bonds and equities.

5. Bonds reclaim macro and hedging value 
Rates moved up in 2026, on oil, hikes, AI spending and fiscal deficits. n
Central bank pricing now too hawkish relative to our baseline. n
Bonds should be a better hedge as supply shocks give way to demand stresses. n
Higher running yield, more paths to relief, and cheap valuations argue for higher n
bond allocations. 
Interest rates moved higher in 2026 after a low point on the eve of the Iran war. The 
resultant surge in energy prices and central bank pricing has been the primary catalyst 
across most DM and EM rates markets, but real rates, especially in the US, have 
continued to move higher as investment demand from the AI build-out interacts with 
sustained fiscal deficits. Despite that backdrop, we think that central bank pricing in 
many economies, including the US and the UK, is too hawkish relative to what is likely to 
be delivered. Even with the energy shock, the underlying inflation story is still fairly 
benign, which means that policy rates are likely to peak at lower levels relative to 
2023-2024 and reverse earlier. The risk to this view is that the energy shock extends 
further or inflation remains stickier and the labour market proves more resilient, as it did 
this year. 
Bonds have been a poor hedge in this environment of energy supply shocks, rate hikes

### Page 8

and real rate pressures from private and public sector spending. But we think the case 
for duration has shifted in a more constructive direction for several reasons. First, on a 
static basis, bond yields are now providing a good running yield and a decent spread to 
cash. Second, valuations are cheap (we see UST 10y yields at 4.6% by end-2027), 
positioning is underweight, and there are paths to some shallow rate relief from current 
levels under our benign baseline scenario, and scope for larger moves if AI spending 
decelerates more quickly. Third, the increase in yields has started to create stresses that 
indicate that a higher level of real rates may be difficult to sustain. Bonds are already 
starting to illustrate their value as a hedge against shocks of these types—with Bunds 
rallying as French sovereign spreads widened and USTs rallying as tech stocks came 
under pressure. Although energy spikes remain a near-term risk, we are more positive 
about bond allocations even after the recent rally, in part because of that hedging value.

6. A more cyclical Dollar, Asia’s revaluing currencies 
After tight ranges in 2026, lower inflation, energy prices make for a softer Dollar. n
But a bigger USD swing could come if US tech exceptionalism erodes. n
JPY is a more attractive hedge with ‘team Japan’ focused on limiting Yen weakness. n
CNY appreciation has further to run given undervaluation and a surging trade n
surplus.  
The broad Dollar is finishing 2026 on the strong side of tight ranges, buoyed by a sturdy 
US nominal growth backdrop, higher energy prices, and a shift to more hawkish Fed 
pricing. Under our baseline forecast, all three impulses should fade: alongside trend 
growth we expect inflation in the US to converge towards target, energy prices should 
decline as the Middle East disruptions gradually normalize and we expect the Fed to 
move from hikes to cuts as we head into late 2027 and early 2028. That should make for

### Page 9

a softer Dollar backdrop, and we have pencilled in a roughly 3% fall in the broad 
trade-weighted Dollar in 2027. 
But the real swing factor for FX is likely to be how the peak in AI-related tech spending 
affects US equity returns. Over the past several years, tech-related macro and market 
outperformance in the US has sucked in equity capital flows from the rest of the world 
and has been the primary factor supporting the Dollar’s premium valuation. Our 
baseline forecasts envisage a more even distribution of returns globally rather than US 
underperformance. But if US tech performance erodes meaningfully, that would likely 
come alongside some cyclical underperformance of the Dollar rather than a traditional 
‘safe haven’ bid, as we have already seen in short periods over the past couple of years. 
Instead, we would expect the undervalued Yen to prove a better haven in that scenario, 
given its typical sensitivity to equities and rates falling together. With Japanese policy 
authorities also now focused on limiting further Yen depreciation and encouraging 
inflows into Japanese assets, we think that the Yen should increasingly be a more 
attractive hedge to pro-cyclical portfolios. We also see scope for the Yuan to continue to 
appreciate gradually as China’s trade surplus keeps growing, and forward markets 
underestimate how far this process can run given the deep undervaluation starting 
point. While domestic demand is also very weak, we have argued that China needs both 
currency appreciation and expansionary policy, two instruments to hit two targets, to 
move towards better external and internal balance.

7. Europe & UK—global shocks and local shifts 
European markets have been under pressure from energy ToT, higher global yields n
and sovereign stresses. 
Even if global shocks abate, local concerns still persist until the French election, n
keeping EUR downside attractive.

### Page 10

UK equities are resilient and defensive, Sterling has continued to strengthen. n
UK Gilts may be clearer outperformers given their highest beta to oil prices. n
European macro markets have had a tough year, primarily reflecting a negative terms of
trade shock, rate tightening without an offsetting investment spending boom, and most
recently the reappearance of sovereign stresses, especially in France. Even with the
repricing, there are still challenges: the energy risks are likely to be most acute going

through the winter, and the underlying fiscal issues in France are unlikely to be
adequately addressed until the French election in the Spring of 2027. As a result, using
the Euro as a funder for other crosses has been a popular position, and the skew of risks
and low carry continue to make downside optionality attractive for the near term. While
Europe remains hostage to global shocks, the main paths to relief also run through those
global channels—energy relief would be most helpful, as would lower yields in the rest of

core markets. Together they would also lessen the immediate pressure on fiscal budgets,
even though the underlying primary deficit would still need to be addressed. Any
slowdown in AI-related spending may also show European assets in a better relative light
given a much smaller share of tech in the region’s equity indices.
In those scenarios, the UK may shine even brighter. Gilts have had the highest beta to oil
prices, so a reduction in those pressures may provide the most relief to UK rates and to

the fiscal picture there. UK equity indices also have less tech exposure than the US
(although they do embed significant energy exposure), and so may also prove more
defensive in an AI-related spending slowdown and any associated market pressures. And
while the currency may also benefit from a softer Dollar backdrop, it is already the most
overvalued currency within our G10 coverage. Much has gone right for the Pound in
recent months—a smooth transition to a new Labour government, a ‘do-no-harm’

budget delivery, the BoE shifting to hikes, and encouraging talk of a closer settlement
with the EU. But we think further gains are likely to be more challenging, with our
monetary policy forecasts most dovish to market pricing in the UK.

Goldman Sachs Global Markets Outlook 2027

### Page 11

8. The stress of financing the boom 
AI investment financing has driven credit and equity issuance to cycle records. n
This has been digestible so far, but with discounted pricing needed in credit that has n
led spreads wider. 
A further widening in US spreads looks likely and a higher chance of meaningful n
widening than meaningful tightening. 
Europe offers more risk premium; mortgages and structured credit give some refuge n
from corporate supply. 
Credit and equity issuance has been hitting fresh cycle records, and the financing needs 
of the AI investment boom mean that those needs are likely to build further in 2027. So 
far, that issuance has—for the most part—been digestible, albeit with discounted 
pricing, and the credit underperformance that we expected in 2026 because of these 
dynamics has only really become clear in the last few months. Balance sheets of the 
major borrowers are strong and while free cash flow positions for the hyperscalers have 
deteriorated sharply, the overall corporate sector is much more balanced. We expect 
continued bifurcation between AI-related issuers and “everything else”, with episodic 
indigestion of jumbo AI debt financing leading to wider spreads for AI-related sectors.  
We expect further widening in index-level credit spreads in 2027, though in our base 
case the scope is fairly modest. The risks around that view are skewed though. It is hard 
to see scenarios where US credit spreads tighten sharply (there is more scope for 
tightening in Europe where the risk premium has increased more), and easier to see how 
spreads could move wider, particularly if economic conditions deteriorate more than we 
expect or if investors become more uncomfortable about the profitability of the AI 
investment boom. We expect modest US HY underperformance (decompression) in our

### Page 12

baseline, but the risk is that problems in the left tail of weaker borrowers create more 
turbulent conditions for broader financing. Mortgages and structured credit markets 
may offer some refuge from the heavy corporate supply. From 1998 to 2000, there was 
sustained widening in credit spreads and substantial decoupling between credit and 
equity markets. Without the large capital inflows and goods price deflation from the EM 
crises in 1997 and 1998, we think that kind of divergence will be harder now.

9. EM—resilience and diversification 
2026 is one more year when EM assets have shown resilience/outperformance in the n
face of external shocks. 
But the flip side is less compelling valuations and lower carry, despite friendly macro. n
So the case for EM investing is exposure to high, diversified and resilient yields in EM n
fixed income ... 
... and a regional and sectorally diversified earnings mix in EM equities.  n
EM assets have again demonstrated impressive resilience in 2026 in the face of shocks 
from high energy prices and the spikes in core rates. EM rates have outperformed core 
rates, EM FX total returns are outpacing G9 and EM equities are on track for a narrower 
but still double-digit year after 30% gains in 2025. The upshot of this resilience and 
outperformance is that in many places valuations are less compelling than before. EM 
local rate spreads are tight relative to core rates, EM credit spreads are the tightest in 
years despite attractive all-in yields, and EM FX carry and valuations are firmly 
middle-of-the-road. 
Even though the baseline macro backdrop looks friendly for EM investing and 
carry-harvesting in 2027, less attractive valuations and carry levels mean that it is about 
gaining exposure to high diversified yields in both hard and local currency fixed income. 
In many cases, such as South Africa, Hungary, Colombia and India, those higher yields 
come with improving fiscal and structural reform stories. Likewise, in the case of EM 
equities, while the major markets are not expensive, much of the value in the headline 
index is an artefact of the surge in AI earnings that will be hard to repeat. Rather, the 
case for EM equity length is also about gaining exposure to a diversified earnings mix.

### Page 13

The EM equity index, especially the less concentrated, equal-weighted flavours, provide 
exposure not just to the tech and AI complex in North Asia, but also to cyclical and 
commodity-related growth in South Africa and LatAm, and to larger domestic demand 
stories such as India. And if we see a larger move lower in core rates or the Dollar than 
our baseline expectations, EM local assets provide upside convexity that is harder to 
replicate elsewhere.

10. Growth risks and tail hedges 
A benign baseline, but growth risks now skew to the downside. n
Diversification benefits from bonds, commodities and equity selection. n
Equity index volatility has scope to rise further. n
Downside in rates, USD/JPY, upside in energy and protection in equity options. n
With a steady economic backdrop, our baseline forecasts for 2027 are for positive 
returns across equities and bonds. But with a maturing market cycle and significant 
uncertainty around core drivers of the outlook, we continue to emphasize the value of 
diversification and hedges to protect against key risks. Some of those risks—from higher 
oil prices or sticky core inflation—are likely to push rates higher. But a growing number—
a sharper reversal in the AI boom, cybersecurity fears, AI-related labour-shedding or 
financial stresses—would push both growth and rate expectations lower. The further we 
move into 2027, the more likely it is that the key risks are positive for bonds, as the 
threats from central bank hikes, energy price spikes and the AI investment tailwind 
recede. Diversification remains valuable in this environment. As we have said, the value 
of bonds as a diversifying asset is returning as the skew of risks shifts. And 
commodities—especially energy—continue to play a hedging role for portfolios, 
particularly when implied risk premia fall as they have recently.  
Alongside diversification, direct hedges are worth considering. Positioning for lower 
rates in the US and Japan, or for jointly lower rates and equities are potential ways to 
protect against deeper downside growth tails. In FX, USD/JPY downside may play a 
similar role, given that it generally performs well when rates and equities are both falling

## Document 450: “智力第一次脱离肉身，从劳动要素变成资本要素”！冯骥8000字长文追问：AI创造的财富究竟该归谁？

Source PDF: all report pdf/“智力第一次脱离肉身，从劳动要素变成资本要素”！冯骥8000字长文追问：AI创造的财富究竟该归谁？.pdf
Report source: WallstreetCN (Global)
Date: 2026-11-28
Pages: 6

### Page 1

《⿊神话：悟空》制作⼈冯骥认为，当AI以极低成本复制智⼒，普通⼈的劳动议价权正在
动摇，财富也可能加速向掌握算⼒和模型的少数巨头集中。AI能⼒建⽴在⼈类数千年的⽂
明积累之上，普通⼈同样是这份共同遗产的持有者。“每⼀个⼈都同样⽣⽽⾼贵，AI创造的
基础物质丰裕，理应通过全⺠分红，平等地惠及每⼀个⼈。 ”
摘要：
《⿊神话：悟空》制作⼈、游戏科学创始⼈冯骥的⼀篇8000字⻓⽂，将⼀场关于AI时代财富分
配的深层追问推⾄公众视野。在他看来，这轮AI⾰命的破坏性远超历次⼯业⾰命——不是因为
它消灭了某类岗位，⽽是因为它第⼀次让智⼒本⾝脱离了⾁⾝，从劳动要素变成了可按度计
费、⽆限复制的资本要素。
11⽉27⽇，冯骥在个⼈公众号"冯骥Yocar"发表《后AI时代最重要的问题》，⽂章迅速在社交媒体
刷屏。他在⽂中指出，当资本可以"直接拿电费去批发智⼒"，普通⼈出售⼯时的议价基础便从根
⼦上动摇了。在⽂娱⾏业，这⼀冲击将表现为超级IP赢家通吃与腰部从业者利润消失的双重极
化。
针对财富分配问题，冯骥提出了他的主张：AI的能⼒本质上源⾃全⼈类五千年的⽂明积累，每

⼀个普通⼈都天然持有这份遗产的"原始股"。因此，AI替代⼈⼯所创造的超额社会财富，理应通
过全⺠分红、按⼈头平等分配，⽽⾮沦为少数巨头的收租⼯具。
两个不同：为何这次⼯业⾰命不⼀样
冯骥在⽂中驳斥了"技术进步终将创造更多新岗位"的乐观论调，指出此轮AI⾰命与历次⼯业⾰命
存在两点根本差异。
其⼀，"迁徙的⾼地没了"。过去机器替代的是肌⾁与脚⼒，劳动者尚能向更⾼的脑⼒⼯种转移。
⽽今，算⼒直接攻占的是脑⼒本⾝，"⾝后没有更⾼⼀层的超脑⼒⼯种可供⼏亿⼈集体转移"。新
⾸⻚ 资讯 快讯 ⾏情 ⽇历 APP VIP会员 ⼤师课 ⻅闻历 搜索

### Page 2

⾏业、新赛道或许仍会出现，但若新增需求主要由AI承接，普通⼈靠劳动分享增⻓的通道将越来
越窄。
其⼆，"淘汰的时钟加速了"。冯骥指出，以往⼀项新技术的普及往往需要⼆三⼗年，慢到⾜够⼀
代⼈从容转型。⽽如今，模型吞掉新技能的速度⽐⼈类报班拿证快上⼀个数量级——"⼈还没上
跑道，终点已经⽆了"。
他将这⼀变化的核⼼总结为：智⼒在历史上第⼀次脱离⾁⾝，从劳动要素变成了资本要素。过
去，⽆论多⾼深的学问都⻓在活⽣⽣的⼈脑⾥，⽆法复制，受限于⽣⽼病死，这道⽣理瓶颈撑
起了普通⼈"勤学苦练→掌握技能→换取收⼊→获得尊重"的核⼼社会契约。⼤模型出现后，这道
瓶颈正被逐步砸碎。
⽂娱⾏业的两极：超级IP与"末法时代"
冯骥以同⼀周发⽣的两件事为引——《GTA6》刷新⼈类⽂娱史收⼊纪录，Fable 5.2⽣成的⽹⻚
游戏令他"玩到忘记时间"——将其定义为同⼀件事的⼀体两⾯。

### Page 3

在他看来，AI带来的内容供给侧繁荣，不仅不会削弱头部IP的统治地位，反⽽会加剧它。内容越
多，受众越挑剔，越依赖熟悉的名字和信得过的⼚牌。华语乐坛已预演过这⼀结局：新歌每天
数以⼗万计地上线，但能在全国撑起⼤型体育场巡演的，依然是⼗余年前出道的⽼⾯孔。
他以⼀个思维实验加以说明：在⼈⼈都能⼀键⽣成同等品质游戏的时代，《魔兽世界》的正版官
⽅⽆限服反⽽可能更加⽕爆，原因只有六个字——"选择太多，共识太少"。多年沉淀的内容、情
怀与共同记忆，是任何AI⽣成物短期内⽆法复制的壁垒。
"创作⼯具确实平权了，但分发⼊⼝没有平权，算⼒分配没有平权，分钱的⽅式更没有平权。"冯
骥写道。他预测，未来世界⼤概率只剩两种娱乐：超级IP垄断的"公共仪式"，以及AI直接包办
的"私⼈定制"。腰部创作者的⽣存空间，将在两端的夹击中被彻底压缩。

### Page 4

分配之争：AI红利应归谁
在论及财富分配时，冯骥⾸先追问了AI能⼒的来源。他认为，⼤模型能够写⼩说、做游戏、听懂
⼈类幽微的情感，依赖的是⾃有⽂字以来五千年⼈类积累的海量语料——代码、图画、声⾳、
影像。这笔横跨五千年的共同遗产"从未挂牌收费，训练模型时便被当成了⽆主的空⽓与江⽔"，
但过去未曾收费，绝不意味着没有主⼈。
基于这⼀判断，他提出：每⼀个活在世上的普通⼈，哪怕不会写⼀⾏代码，只要继承⼈类的语
⾔与⽂明秩序，就天然持有这份遗产不可剥夺的"原始股"。当通⽤⼈⼯智能从实验室产品变成像
⽔、电、道路⼀样的基础设施，其⻓期红利就理应回流给全社会。
他的具体主张是：在坚持按劳分配为主体的前提下，将由AI替代⼈⼯所创造的超额物质财富，
平等地分配每⼀个⼈。他将此与硅⾕常谈的全⺠基本收⼊（UBI）加以区分——后者在某些语境
下是技术领主对失业者的"施舍"，⽽他主张的分配，是全体⽂明股东"天经地义的⽆差别分红"。

市场报价"衡量⼈的价值。他认为，这套规则有⼀个不⾔⾃明的前提——⽣产离不开⼈，⼈的贡

## Document 519: 中国製ハイブリッド車の輸入制限に踏み切るEU

Source PDF: all report pdf/中国製ハイブリッド車の輸入制限に踏み切るEU.pdf
Report source: MURC
Date: 2026-10-20
Pages: 4
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

2026 年 10 月 20 日

MURC Focus

中国製ハイブリッド車の輸入制限に踏み切る EU

～日本にとっても“対岸の火事”ではない～

調査部 主任研究員 土田 陽介

1. 12 月にセーフガードを発動 
欧州連合（EU）は 10 月 12 日、輸入が急増している中国製のハイブリッド車（HV）とプラグインハイブリッド車
（PHV）を対象に、12 月よりセーフガード（緊急輸入制限）を発動すると発表した。期間は 4 年間で、1 年目は HV
と PHV の年間輸入台数を計 35 万台とし、その後の増加幅は年間 6%までに制限する。EU 籍のメーカーが中
国で生産する車も対象となるが、中国メーカーの EU 域内での生産車は対象外となる。

EU は 2024 年 10 月 30 日より、中国製 EV（電気自動車）に対し、基本関税 10%に加えて、最大で 35.3%の
追加関税を課している。廉価な中国製 EV の輸入を抑制する目的からだが、一方で HV と PHV には追加関税
を課してこなかった。その結果、中国製 HV と PHV の輸入が 2025 年から急増し、EU 製の HV と PHV は劣勢
に追いやられた（図表 1）。また電動車の対中貿易収支も、HV と PHV を中心に赤字が膨らんだ（図表 2）。 
図表 1. 中国製電動車の輸入台数の推移 図表 2. 電動車の対中貿易収支

（注）EV:電気自動車、PHV:プラグインハイブリッド車、HV: ハイブリッド車

〇EU は10月12日、輸入が急増している中国製の HV と PHV を対象に、12月よりセーフガード（緊急輸入制
限）を発動すると発表した。 
〇2025年に再登板した米国のドナルド・トランプ大統領による貿易・関税政策を保護主義だと批判する EU だ
が、その EU もまた保護主義の性格を強めている。 
〇今回の中国製 HV と PHV に対するセーフガードは、日本にとって“対岸の火事”ではない。政府と日系メーカ
ーが一丸となり、EU に対して互恵主義に基づく平等な競争条件の順守を訴えていくことが望まれる。

### Page 2

EU の 2025 年の中国製 HV と PHV の輸入台数の実績値は計 30 万台であったが、2026 年の輸入台数は
80 万台に上ると予測されている（1～7 月期の実績値は約 38 万台）。通商担当であるマロシュ・シェフチョビッチ
欧州委員は今回の措置で、2027 年の中国製 HV と PHV の輸入台数（計 35 万台）は 2026 年から半減すると
アピールしている。

とはいえ EU は、中国製 HV と PHV の輸入台数が年間 6%まで増加することを容認しているため、2027 年の
輸入台数が計 35 万台に抑制されても、セーフガード最終年の 2030 年の輸入台数は計 42 万台まで容認するこ
とになる。要するに EU は、中国との全面的な対立を回避するために、EU 市場に対する一定のマーケットアクセ
スを容認していることになる。

2. 特に中国に対して保護主義傾向を強める EU 
このように、2025 年に再登板した米国のドナルド・トランプ大統領による貿易・関税政策を保護主義だと批判す
る EU もまた、保護主義の性格を強めているが、背景には国際競争力の低下がある。一例が価格競争力の指標
である実質実効為替レートで、人民元と日本円とで比較すると、ユーロの増価が鮮明である（図表 3）。これは欧
州中銀（ECB）による高金利政策のみならず、EU の高インフレに伴う内外価格差の拡大に起因する。

高インフレが生じた理由は、そのエネルギー政策（ロシア産の安価なエネルギーの排除とそれに伴う再生可能
エネルギーへのシフト）に加えて、賃金政策（最低賃金の引上げなどに起因する物価・賃金スパイラル）の影響に
よるところが大きい。それ以外にも、EU の執行部局である欧州委員会が環境面を中心に、いわゆる“過剰規制”
を課したことが産業競争力の低下を招いたと指摘される。

図表 3. 実質実効為替レートの比較 図表 4. EU の貿易収支

### Page 3

競争力を失った EU の貿易収支は安定した黒字を稼ぐことができなくなり、直近 2026 年 4～6 月期は▲220
億ユーロと、イラン発のエネルギーショックに伴うエネルギー価格高騰のあおりもあって 2023 年 1～3 月期以来
の赤字になった（図表 4）。特に EU が抱える対中貿易赤字は巨額であり、EU はその削減を重視している。今回
の中国製 HV と PHV に対するセーフガードの発動も、こうした動きの延長線上にある。

EU は雇用確保の観点から、中国企業に対して、輸出を増やすのではなく EU 域内で生産をするように求めて
いる。そのための直接投資（FDI）の受け入れに EU は積極的だが、同時に EU は防衛やインフラなど戦略産業
に関しては中国による投資や中国製品を排除する意向を鮮明にしている。いずれにせよ、米国に比べれば穏や
かとはいえ、EU もまた、とりわけ中国を念頭に保護主義の動きを強めている。

3. 日本も EU の保護主義対象に 
今回の EU の措置は、あくまで中国製 HV と PHV に対するものである。そのため、他国製 HV と PHV には
適用されない。日系自動車メーカーもまた日本から、あるいはトルコの生産拠点などから EU 向けに自動車（主に
HV）を輸出しているが、これらの HV と PHV は今回のセーフガードの対象にならない（図表 5）。ただし、結局の
ところ、EU の保護主義は先行き、日本にも及んでくることが懸念される。

実際、欧州委員会が 2026 年 3 月に提案した産業加速化法（IAA）に基づき、EU が公共調達や公的支援で
域内製品の優遇を図る際には、日本や韓国、トルコなどの友好国から輸入した自動車などはその対象から排除さ
れると報道されている。また日系を含めた非 EU 籍のメーカーが EU 域内で生産した自動車も、EU 域内のメー
カーからの部品調達がなされない限り、同様に IAA の対象から排除される方向にある。

### Page 4

日本と EU は 2019 年 2 月 1 日に経済連携協定（日 EU・EPA）を発効しており、互恵主義に基づく自由で公
正な貿易と投資を尊重し合う関係にある。そして日本も、輸入を中心に対 EU 貿易を拡大してきた（図表 6）。しか
しながら、このように自由主義に基づく通商関係を確認した日本に対しても、EU は結局のところ、保護主義の性
格を強めつつある。

こうした傾向は、ウルズラ・フォン・デア・ライエン欧州委員長を中心とする現体制が続く限りは修正が望みにく
い。このように整理していくと、今回の中国製 HV と PHV に対するセーフガードは、日本にとって “対岸の火事”
ではない。EU が日本に対して不当競争を強いることがないよう、政府と日系メーカーが一丸となり、EU に対して
互恵主義に基づく平等な競争条件の順守を訴えていくことが望まれるところである。

## Document 539: 低利率环境下商业银行利率衍生品的双账簿应用与策略

Report source: 金融市场研究

Pages: 10
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

VOL.173 2026.10《金融市场研究》
89
段兵  |  低利率环境下商业银行利率衍生品的双账簿应用与策略
低利率环境下商业银行利率衍生品的双账簿
应用与策略
段兵
摘  要  近年来我国市场利率中枢持续下移，商业银行面临的久期、重定价和多曲线基差风险更
加凸显，利率衍生品在投资交易与资产负债管理中的作用随之上升。本文从交易账簿与银行账簿双维度，
剖析利率衍生品在风险对冲、收益增强、代客服务领域的具体应用场景与实操策略。当前业务发展仍
受中长期市场流动性不足、套期会计实施成本较高、机构能力分化及应用模式偏窄等因素制约。推动
衍生品管理嵌入资产负债管理，完善产品供给、市场深度、套期会计操作机制和分类监管，有助于提
升商业银行利率风险管理的精细化水平。
关键词  利率衍生品  利率互换  国债期货  利率风险管理
段兵，中国农业银行金融市场部总裁。
1　商业银行净息差由2023年1.69%回落至2026年一季度1.40%，创历史低位，大行净息差仅1.29%。
2　根据财政部《地方政府债券市场报告》，2025年 ，我 国 发 行 10年期及以上地方政府债券77 180.60亿 元 ，占 比

74.86%，远 高 于 2019年46.83%的占比。商业银 行作为主要配置 主体，持仓占比超六成，债券组合久 期中枢明显
抬 升。
伴随国内低利率环境常态化，商业银
行净息差不断收窄，传统息差盈利模式持
续承压 1。为适配市场环境、增厚资产收益，
商业银行普遍增配长久期债券与固定利率
信贷资产，资产久期显著拉长，存量资产
利率风险持续累积 2。一旦市场利率触底反
弹，商业银行存量长久期资产将面临估值
大幅回撤、重定价滞后风险，易造成经营
亏损。传统利率风险管理方式主要依靠调
整信贷投放结构、盘活存量现券等手段，
存在调整时滞长、交易成本较高、风险覆
盖不够全面等局限。相较之下，利率衍生
品资金占用较低，能够灵活、高效调整久
期与重定价缺口，现已成为商业银行实施
精细化资产负债管理、对冲低利率周期经
营压力的核心工具。
一、利率衍生品主要类型与市场发展
情况
（一）利率衍生品主要类型
利率衍生品是以市场利率为标的、价
值随利率波动变化的金融工具，核心作用
是剥离、转移和对冲利率风险，主要包括

利率互换、国债期货、债券远期、远期利
率协议、利率期权等，可从交易场所、收
益风险特征、挂钩标的三个维度展开分类。
一是一是按交易场所分为场内衍生品与场
外衍生品。场内衍生品为交易所统一挂牌
的标准化合约，流动性强、信用风险低，
核心品种为国债期货，主要满足银行短期、
标准化的组合久期调整需求，适配交易账
簿日常风控。场外衍生品为双边协商的定

### Page 2

90
《金融市场研究》2026.10 VOL.173
金融评论
Financial Review
制化合约，灵活性更高，主要包括利率互
换、远期利率协议、债券远期等，可精准
匹配银行中长期信贷、固息债券的个性化
风险敞口，适用于银行账簿中长期利率风
险与息差保护。
二是二是按收益风险特征分为线性衍生品
与非线性衍生品。线性衍生品收益与标的
利率呈对称变动关系，结构简单、交易成
熟，涵盖国债期货、利率互换、远期利率
协议等，是银行常态化对冲利率波动、稳
定净息差的基础工具，适配低利率环境下
稳健风控需求。非线性衍生品以利率期权
为代表，收益结构非对称，具备风险有限、
可保留行情收益的特点，银行可通过构建
区间保护组合，在锁定息差下行风险的同
时把握利率上行收益，适配震荡行情下的
精细化风控与收益增厚需求。
三是三是按挂钩标的分为多基准衍生品体
系。依据挂钩基准利率的差异，衍生品可
适配银行多维风险敞口：货币市场基准衍
生品以 FR007、Shibor 为标的，以短期互
换、远期利率协议为主，用于对冲同业负

债等短端利率风险；信贷市场基准衍生品
以 LPR 为核心标的，匹配信贷资产重定价
需求，是低利率环境下稳定贷款收益、缓
解息差下行压力的关键工具；国债收益率
基准衍生品包含国债期货、债券远期，主
要用于管控利率债组合的久期与估值风
险。多基准工具组合运用，可有效应对低
利率环境下多曲线分化引发的基差风险。
上述三类分类维度相互交叉，实务中
商业银行会结合交易场所、收益风险特征、
挂钩基准利率综合遴选工具，匹配差异化
1　 数据来源于央行官网。
的利率风险敞口。
（二）我国利率衍生品市场发展
经过多年发展，国内利率衍生品市场
持续扩容、产品体系不断丰富，商业银行
常态化开展利率风险对冲有了市场基础。
一是市场规模稳步扩容，参与主体日一是市场规模稳步扩容，参与主体日
趋多元趋多元。2025 年，银行间场外利率衍生品
成交名义本金达 58.5 万亿元，同比增长
58.6%，国债期货成交 97.0 万亿元，同比
增长 43.9%，市场活跃度持续提升。1 商业
银行、券商、基金、保险都已参与，商业

银行是主要的做市和流动性供给方。金融
机构间的标准利率互换实行强制集中清算
后，对手方信用约束减轻，机构参与的门
槛随之降低。
二是产品体系持续丰富，需求适配二是产品体系持续丰富，需求适配
度不断提升度不断提升。国内利率衍生品已形成多品
类、全期限产品矩阵。场外市场以利率互
换为核心，根据交易商协会发布的《中国
场外金融衍生品市场发展报告（2025 年 ）》，
2025 年主力品种利率互换（IRS）交易名
义本金达 54.3 万亿元，占场外利率衍生品
交易总量九成以上，FR007 基准互换成为
短期常态化风控主力工具。场内市场覆盖
2 年、5 年、10 年、30 年全期限国债期货，
2024 年成交 67.4 万亿元，同比增长 20.1%，
2025 年成交规模大幅扩张至 97.0 万亿元，
增长动能强劲。同时标准债券远期、同业
存单互换等创新品种快速扩容，2025 年标
准债券远期成交名义本金 3.61 万亿元。
三是制度体系日趋完善，市场运行更三是制度体系日趋完善，市场运行更
加规范加规范。我国利率衍生品坚持制度先行，

律 + 基础设施规范”的立体化制度体系。
2022 年《期货和衍生品法》落地，补齐衍

风险防控机制。央行、金融监管总局、交

专项规则，套期会计则由财政部《企业会
计准则第 24 号——套期会计》规范，与
IFRS 9 趋同。上海清算所集中清算机制全
面落地，有效缓释对手方信用风险，推动
市场由规模扩容向提质增效转型。
四是对外开放稳步深化，国际化水平四是对外开放稳步深化，国际化水平
持续提升持续提升。国内利率衍生品市场国际化布
局持续推进，北向互换通运行以来，境外
机构可直接参与境内利率互换交易。2025
年北向互换通全年清算规模 5.3 万亿元。1
境外机构的参与增加了互换市场的对手方
和定价视角，境内银行的报价也直接面对
国际同业。
值得注意的是，伴随国内低利率环境
持续演进，商业银行资产负债端中长期利

率风险敞口快速扩张，银行账簿真实套保
需求显著抬升，但当前市场交易仍集中于
短期限标准化品种，中长期对冲工具供给
不足的矛盾逐步凸显，制约衍生品服务银
行利率风险管理的效能。
二、利率衍生品在商业银行中的业务
场景与应用策略
商业银行利率衍生品业务可分为风险
管理、收益增强、代客服务三大核心场景，
各场景在账簿归属、交易逻辑、风控要求
与会计处理上形成明确区分。这里的交易
账簿、银行账簿是《商业银行资本管理办
1　 数据来源于Wind。
法》按持有目的划分的监管账簿，与会计
上的金融资产分类并不一一对应：银行账
簿既有以摊余成本计量的贷款和债券，也
有计入其他综合收益的债券。账簿归属决
定了风险指标、会计处理和可用的工具。
（一）风险对冲场景：双账簿差异化套
期实践
商业银行往往利用会计重新分类来
减轻利率上升对账面资本的影响，而没
有充分对冲银行资产下跌的市场价值风险
（Granja et al.，2024）。利率风险管理失效
是 20 世纪 80 年代储贷危机以及 2023 年
美国区域性银行危机的重要诱因（Chen

et al.，2025 ；Jiang et al.，2024）。风险对
冲核心目标不是消灭利率风险，而是将利
率风险的形态、暴露时点、损益实现方式
进行改造，分别适配交易账簿“管控估值
波动”与银行账簿“管控净息差波动”两
类目标。
交易账簿套期实践交易账簿套期实践。交易账簿主要持
仓为交易类利率债，债券按公允价值计量，
利率变动直接反映在当期损益。实操中银
行一般以组合 DV01、修正久期作为核心
计量指标，并按关键期限拆分，先计量自
营债券组合整体利率风险敞口，再通过衍
生品对冲目标缺口。利率上行阶段，做空
国债期货或买入收浮动付固定的短期利率
互换，压低组合久期，缓释债券价格下跌
带来的浮亏；利率下行阶段适度降低对冲
比例，保留现券价格上涨的收益。从会计
层面，交易性债券与衍生品均按公允价值
计量、变动均计入当期损益，两者自然抵
消，一般无须指定套期关系；对于计入其

### Page 4

92
《金融市场研究》2026.10 VOL.173
金融评论
Financial Review
他综合收益的债券，若完成套期关系指定，
衍生品与被套期债券公允价值变动相互抵
消，有效部分平抑利润波动；未指定套期
关系，则衍生品损益计入当期损益而债券
估值变动留在其他综合收益，会计错配反
而放大利润波动。实务难点集中在基差扰
动：国债期货的隐含基差、互换现券基差
会随资金面、市场情绪发生变化，即便久
期完全匹配，仍然会产生套期无效部分。
国债期货基差还会随最便宜可交割券切换
和交割月临近而变化，因此交易台通常把
期货对冲当作调整久期的工具，不指望其
精确复制现券。近年“互换-现券”基差对
套期效果的扰动整体大于国债期货基差。
交割月资金成本、CTD 切换属于阶段性、
时点性冲击，但由于有交割硬约束，基差
不会长期偏离合理区间，收敛确定性强。
而低利率环境下银行配置盘集中增配长久
期债券，容易造成互换端定价错位，该类
基差偏离不存在强制收敛机制，对中长期
套保效果干扰更为突出。
银行账簿套期实践银行账簿套期实践。银行账簿资产负

债多以摊余成本计量，风险体现为未来利
息现金流波动，不直接产生估值损益，风
险指标重点关注净利息收入（NII）与经济
价值（EVE）。实操模式分为两类：一是一是负
债端保护，当预判负债成本上行，签订“收
浮动、付固定”中长期利率互换，锁定未
来付息成本；二是二是资产端保护，当预判浮
动利率资产收益率下行，签订“收固定、
付浮动”互换，锁定资产端利息流入。震
荡行情中，部分头部银行会进一步搭配利
率期权组合，设置利率上下限，既锁定息
差下行风险，又保留利率上行带来的盈利
改善空间。不过境内利率期权市场规模尚
小，这类操作目前仍属个案。会计上适用
现金流量套期现金流量套期，套期有效部分计入其他综
合收益 OCI，跨周期递延，待到被套期项
目影响损益时再重分类进利润表，以此平
滑多年度净息差；无效部分直接计入当期
损益。固定利率的长久期债券和贷款若以
付固定互换对冲，适用的则是公允价值套
期，有效性评估和损益处理均不相同，指
定前需先行明确。在低利率常态化背景下，
银行账簿积累大量长久期固定利率信贷与

债券资产，目前最常见被套期项目是固定
利率债券。该场景实务门槛很高：需要建
立完整被套期项目台账，持续开展套期有
效性评估，区分有效 / 无效部分。很多机
构能开展交易，但很难持续产出符合审计
要求的有效性材料；一旦有效性不满足，
套期会计暂停，浮亏浮盈直接进损益，冲
击利润，业务不敢落地。
银行使用衍生品开展套期的意愿，在
很大程度上取决于现行会计准则下获取
套期会计指定资格的难易程度（Ali et al.，
2026）。以美国为例，2017 年之前，依据《美
国财务会计准则第 133 号 》（ SFAS 133），
获取套期会计指定需要承担高昂的报告与
合规成本。为防范企业利用衍生品开展机
会主义交易，SFAS 133 对套期会计指定
设置了严苛且复杂的要求（Leone，2007
和 2008 ；Makar et al.， 2013 ；Campbell，
2015 ；Chang et al.，2016 ；Khan et al.，
2018）。 2017 年，美国财务会计准则委
员 会（FASB）发布《会计准则更新第

2017-12 号 》（ ASU 2017-12），就是为了降
低长期以来制约银行运用套期会计的操作
层面与会计层面摩擦因素。国内的情况类
似：《企业会计准则第 24 号——套期会计》
已不再要求过去那种量化的高度有效测
试，改为经济关系、信用风险不占主导和
